## Post-audit: remaining-work sweep + full green suite (2026-07-22)

### Code remaining for the two plans: NONE

Reliability Substrate Plan 2 and the Tier-2 sharding buildout are both code-complete, wired and
tested. The only Plan-2 items still open were **dropped by owner decision** (2026-07-21), not deferred:
the external underwriter seam (was L1.2) and the indicative premium / cross-tenant actuarial aggregate
(was L1.3). Nothing is queued behind them.

### Fixed: the agentschedule/temporalstore suite was genuinely broken, not "environmental"

The earlier audit wrote this off as a pre-existing flake. That was too generous. The suite had **zero
polling anywhere**: it called `store.Ensure(...)` and then immediately read back, including via
`insp.ListScheduleRuns`, which reads Temporal's **visibility** store and is indexed out-of-band. Every
assertion raced the write that produced it, which is why a different subset failed on each run.

By `CLAUDE.md §7.2` a flaky test is a bug, so it was fixed rather than tolerated. All post-write
assertions now poll to convergence with `require.Eventually` (never a fixed sleep), with two details
worth keeping:

- The **update** assertion now waits for the new **overlap policy** specifically. The old assertion
  waited only for "a cadence exists", which the pre-update state already satisfied, so it proved
  nothing about the update having landed.
- `PreservePauseIfExists` is a **must-NOT-change** property, so it is asserted with `require.Never`
  over a hold window. A single immediate read could have passed merely because the update had not
  landed yet.

Closure bodies deliberately contain no `require`/`assert`: testify runs an Eventually condition on its
own goroutine, where `t.FailNow` is undefined.

Result: 5 previously-failing tests pass, stable across `-count=3`, and **`go test -tags=integration
-p 1 ./...` is now fully green with no exclusions** (previously it always had this package failing).

### Infra/manual work: consolidated into the deployment runbook

Per the standing rule that `infra/docs/Actrone_Deployment_Runbook.md` is the single "what's left to
deploy" source:

- **Rewrote §8.2 (Tier 2).** It still described the pre-build world ("code-ready, config to light up",
  "reuse the existing region-cutover primitive"). It now documents what actually exists: shard naming
  `<region>-<n>` and the region-nesting guard, schema fan-out via `internal/dbmigrate.FanOut`,
  directory-first placement with fail-fast bootstrap and live LISTEN/NOTIFY propagation, the governed
  move SAGA with parity-verify and reverse-move rollback, and the advisor endpoints. It points at
  `infra/docs/Actrone_Shard_TurnUp_Runbook.md` for the procedure and lists the alerts to watch.
- **Added §6.22 (Reliability Substrate Plan 2).** Previously absent entirely. Covers: attestation
  reuses the GAL signing key so it lights up with GAL and fails closed (503) without it, key rotation
  invalidates offline verification of already-issued attestations (publish the old key alongside the
  new), migration `00134` before volume use, the ~N x token cost of speculative execution and its
  hard cap of 8 branches, granting the `auditor` role in WorkOS as the real access boundary for PII
  reveal, the 32-byte `ORCHESTRATOR_EMAOP_MAL_TOKEN_KEY` requirement, the 365-day retention ceiling,
  and the fact that `replay-edit` forks rather than resets (so it creates a new task that shows up in
  counts and metering).
- **Added two alert rules** to the `actrone.sharding` group: `ShardScatterSweepPartial` and
  `ShardScatterSweepFailing`. The audit added the `shard_scatter_total` metric and called a partial
  sweep "alertable" without actually adding the alert; it is now real. YAML validated.

### Repo hygiene note

A `Get-Content` + `Set-Content` round-trip in PowerShell 5.1 double-encoded the deployment runbook
(UTF-8 read as ANSI, rewritten as UTF-8), producing 206 mojibake sequences. Repaired losslessly via a
CP1252 round-trip and rewritten without a BOM; verified zero mojibake and zero replacement characters.
**Do not edit repo files with `Get-Content`/`Set-Content` in Windows PowerShell**, use the editing
tools directly.

`go build ./...` OK. Full unit suite green. **`go test -tags=integration -p 1 ./...` fully green** with
Postgres 16 in Docker plus Temporal dev servers. TS SDK typecheck clean, 189/189. Python SDK `mypy`
clean (57 files), 266 passed / 21 skipped. Control Tower `tsc --noEmit` clean. Alert rules parse.

All changes remain **uncommitted**.

## Platform-wide code-completeness sweep (2026-07-22)

The earlier audits were grep-driven and produced at least one garbage result (a probe for "SCIM"
matched numpy's `scimath`). This pass used **reachability analysis** instead, which is mechanical and
does not depend on knowing what to look for:

1. `go list ./internal/...` vs `go list -deps ./cmd/...` for **package**-level reachability.
2. `golang.org/x/tools/cmd/deadcode` over all 6 binaries for **function**-level reachability.
3. Every candidate then verified by hand, because reachability tools cut both ways (see the two
   correction notes below).

### Package-level: 131 of 134 internal packages reachable

| Package | Verdict |
| --- | --- |
| `internal/shardguard` | Correct. Test-only lint (the `Primary()` audit guard); it has no production caller by design. |
| `internal/projection` | Correct, deliberately dormant. The CQRS projector primitive awaits a concrete read model, which is chosen from observed read pressure. **Runbook §8.4 said "not yet in-repo", which was stale, and has been corrected.** |
| `internal/loopeval` | **Real gap.** The LE0 "measure first" loop-quality harness is an island: `TurnRecord` appears nowhere except `loopeval` itself, so nothing produces the telemetry it scores. LE0's premise (prove every later loop change against a baseline) currently cannot be exercised. |

### Function-level: 250 unreachable funcs, triaged

Most are legitimate exported API. The clusters that mattered:

- **`internal/voice` (53) is a SUPERSEDED implementation, not a missing feature.** `RealtimeBridge`,
  `DialProvider`, `NewTwilioCallerAudio` and `InMemoryAssignmentQueue` have zero production call sites
  (`DialProvider` and `NewTwilioCallerAudio` have none at all, not even tests). Voice itself IS wired:
  production goes `TwilioHandler` → `TelephonyFactory.Begin` → `voice.NewVoiceSession`. So this is a
  stale alternative bridge. It should be deleted: it is dead weight that actively misleads, and it
  briefly misled this very audit into thinking voice was unwired.
- **`internal/onboarding` (7) is unwired.** `Service`, `CreateSandboxSession`, `TeardownSandbox` and
  `Checklist` are never constructed. Note the *templates* handler (`NewOnboardingHandler`) IS mounted,
  so this is specifically the sandbox/checklist service, not onboarding as a whole.
- **`internal/distill` (10): the serving path is live, two sub-components are not.** `distill` is wired
  via `cmd/orchestrator/distill_adapters.go`, but `BoundedFineTuner` and `FailoverServer` (plus their
  breaker) have no production call site.
- **`dbmigrate.Parity` has no caller and no test.** This is the shard migration-version-skew check.
  Once sharded, an operator has no wired way to detect that one shard is behind on migrations, which is
  exactly the condition that makes a move corrupt data.
- **`regionpool.ScatterCount`** still has no production caller (`Scatter` is now wired; the count
  wrapper is not).
- **`shardbalance.Recommend` / `TopTenants`** remain library-only. `Recommend` is deliberately not
  HTTP-exposed (it names other tenants' ids, which must not cross an org boundary), but "deliberately
  not exposed" and "unused" are the same thing in practice, and it is honest to say so.

### Gap found in my OWN work and fixed: P4 placement was never wired

The earlier audit closed P4's *advisory* half (loads + advice endpoints) and declared P4 complete. It
was not. **`Balancer.PlaceNewTenant` had zero call sites**, so a brand-new tenant got no directory row
and was placed by consistent hash. Hash-placed tenants are precisely the ones a later shard add
reshuffles, so the claim that "adding a shard moves nobody" was not actually true for any tenant
created after turn-up.

Fixed by wiring placement into both tenant-birth paths (`IdentityService.OnOrgCreated` and
`ensurePersonalWorkspace`) behind a new optional `ShardPlacer` seam, with an adapter
(`cmd/orchestrator/shard_placer.go`) that resolves residency region first. Design points:

- **Best-effort, never fatal.** A signup that dies because the shard directory blipped is far worse
  than a tenant that falls back to hash routing, which still routes correctly. Failures are logged with
  the concrete remediation (backfill the row before adding a shard) and the existing bootstrap coverage
  guard already reports the resulting gap.
- **Never guesses a residency plane.** Residency is chosen after the org exists, so when the region
  cannot be resolved the tenant is deliberately left unplaced. Pinning it to a cluster outside the plane
  it later lands in would be refused at route time by the region-nesting guard and would alert forever.
- **Sentinels, not error-string matching.** Added `shardbalance.ErrAlreadyPlaced` and
  `ErrNoShardInRegion` so the caller can treat both as benign without parsing messages.
- **Latent bug fixed on the way:** `PlaceNewTenant` treated *any* error from the existing-placement
  read as "no row", so a transient database failure would have placed an already-placed tenant onto a
  second shard and split its data across two clusters. It now distinguishes `pgx.ErrNoRows` from a real
  read failure.
- Adapter depends on a `tenantPlacer` interface so the outcome translation is unit-tested without a
  database (4 tests, table-driven over the benign outcomes plus a real-failure case).

### Two corrections to how these tools were read

- **`deadcode` reported all `shardbalance` functions reachable after the fix, including `Recommend`.**
  That is RTA widening: once the concrete type is reached through an interface, more of its methods get
  marked reachable. Verified by hand instead: `Recommend` still has 0 production call sites. Reachability
  output is a lead, not a verdict.
- **Build tags were ruled out before concluding anything.** `internal/` has no `//go:build` files
  outside tests, and all 6 `cmd/` binaries were analysed, so the unreachable results are not an artifact
  of an unanalysed build configuration.

### Not covered by this sweep

This is a **Go-backend** reachability sweep. It says nothing about unwired frontend routes/components,
Python or TypeScript SDK surface, or features that exist and are reachable but are incomplete against
their plan. Those need their own passes and should not be inferred from this one.

### Verification

`go build ./...` OK. `gofmt` clean for touched files. Full unit suite green. **`go test -tags=integration
-p 1 ./...` fully green** against Postgres 16 in Docker.

All changes remain **uncommitted**.

## Closing the sweep's gaps (2026-07-22)

Working through the unreachable-code findings. Reachability went **250 to 234** unreachable functions,
and every closure below is wired, tested, and green.

### 1. Shard schema-drift detection is now continuous, not a helper nobody calls

`dbmigrate.Parity` had no caller and no test. That mattered more than it looks: a shard left behind on
the migration chain keeps serving traffic perfectly well while having a **different table shape**, and
the move's own parity verify compares row counts and checksums, so it **cannot** catch DDL drift.

- `dbmigrate.Watcher` samples parity on an interval (default 5 min) and publishes
  `shard_schema_version{target}`, `shard_schema_drift`, `shard_schema_check_total{result}`.
- **"Unknown" is never reported as "no drift".** If a target cannot be read, the drift gauge deliberately
  holds its last value rather than reassuring you with a zero. A watcher that reassures while blind is
  worse than no watcher.
- `GET /v1/admin/shards/schema` is the on-demand pre-flight before a move; an incomplete read answers
  **503**, not a misleading 200.
- Alerts `ShardSchemaDrift` (critical) and `ShardSchemaCheckFailing` (warning).
- `migrationTargets` is now shared by the startup fan-out and the watcher, so the two cannot disagree
  about which databases are meant to match.
- 4 new tests, including the unreadable-targets case.

### 2. The cross-tenant rebalance advisor now has a home: the Super Admin Dashboard

`shardbalance.Recommend` was library-only. It could not go on the customer API because its
recommendations name **other tenants' ids**. The SAD is exactly the right plane (separate binary,
private bind, isolated DB, never on a customer request path), and it already holds a read-only customer
pool, which is all the advisor needs since load is computed entirely from the control plane.

- `GET /api/v1/shards/loads` and `/shards/recommendations`, gated on `databases:read`, audited.
- New `ADMIN_SHARD_IDS`. Configured rather than derived from the directory on purpose: a freshly
  provisioned shard has no tenants, so deriving the set would hide exactly the empty shard you want to
  rebalance **onto**.
- Query params are allowlist-validated and **rejected** when out of range rather than clamped, so an
  operator is never handed advice computed under a policy they did not ask for and might act on.

### 3. `regionpool.ScatterCount` removed

A tested convenience wrapper with no consumer. `Scatter` covers everything it did, and both production
sweeps return rows rather than a single number. Rather than invent a caller to justify it, it is gone;
the integration test now exercises `Scatter` directly, so the coverage of partial results, empty pool
sets, and merge-in-Go is preserved. Speculative API is the thing that made this audit hard.

### 4. Onboarding state machine + checklist wired (the sandbox half is a CONTENT gap, not a wiring gap)

The pure state graph and checklist existed with no persistence and no routes; the handler's own comment
anticipated this ("the sandbox/state endpoints layer on once their provisioner + state store are wired").

- `repository.OnboardingRepository` over `onboarding_states` (migration 00034 already existed).
- `Advance` holds a **transaction with the row locked**: two events arriving together (a connector
  webhook and a UI click) would otherwise both read the same state and the second would silently
  overwrite the first, losing a transition. Covered by a concurrent integration test.
- An illegal transition is rejected **and writes nothing**, verified by test.
- Routes: `GET /v1/onboarding/state`, `POST /events`, `PUT /checklist`, `PUT /vertical`, each scoped to
  the caller's own user (taken from the session, never the request). An API key is rejected, because
  onboarding is a per-user journey and silently picking a user would attribute one person's progress to
  another.
- New `IsKnownEvent` / `NextEvents` / `IsKnownVertical`, all derived from the transition graph so
  validation cannot drift behind a new transition. This lets the API separate a **typo (400)** from a
  legal event sent at the wrong time (**409**), which are different client bugs.
- 5 unit tests + 4 integration tests.

**Still open (content, not code):** `Service.CreateSandboxSession` / `TeardownSandbox` remain unwired
because there is no `SandboxProvisioner` implementation and **no per-vertical synthetic datasets**
(hr/finance/it/procurement). Wiring the service without datasets would provision an empty schema, which
is a fake feature. Building those datasets is product content.

### 5. Distillation: the two resilience wrappers are now actually in the path

`BoundedFineTuner` and `FailoverServer` were built, tested, and unused, meaning the running system had
neither the concurrency bound (§3.4) nor the circuit breaker (§4.4) those standards require.

- Fine-tune submissions now go through `BoundedFineTuner` (`ORCHESTRATOR_COST_OPT_MAX_CONCURRENT_FINE_TUNES`,
  default 8). Eligibility is evaluated per agent, so one sweep can make many tenants eligible at once;
  unbounded, that stampedes the provider API and the spend with it.
- Serving goes through `FailoverServer` with a per-provider breaker. An optional second provider is
  registered only when `ORCHESTRATOR_MODEL_FINE_TUNE_FALLBACK_{BASE_URL,API_KEY}` are both set, so the
  default single-provider setup is unchanged.

**Root cause of why it was never wired, now fixed:** `FailoverServer.Complete` returned a **fourth**
value and therefore did **not** implement `ServingProvider`, so it was impossible to drop in where the
interface's own doc comment said it belonged. The 4-value form is now `CompleteWithProvider`, a
conforming `Complete` and `Name()` were added, and a `var _ ServingProvider = (*FailoverServer)(nil)`
assertion means a future signature change fails to **compile** rather than silently making it unwirable
again. Serving via a fallback logs a warning, since serving still works and would otherwise go unnoticed.

### Verification

`go build ./...` OK, `gofmt` clean for touched files, **full unit suite green**, and
**`go test -tags=integration -p 1 ./...` fully green** against Postgres 16 in Docker plus Temporal dev
servers. All changes remain **uncommitted**.

### Deliberately NOT done, and why

- **`distill.DecideRollback`** (the quality circuit-breaker that reverts a regressed distilled model to
  the frontier) is still uncalled. This is a **real safety gap**: a promoted model that degrades keeps
  serving. Closing it is not wiring, it needs new work: a promoted-model listing, a live pass-rate query
  over a monitoring window, a standalone demote path, resolver-cache invalidation, and a periodic
  monitor. Roughly 250 lines plus tests.
- **`loopeval`** is an **offline** scorer by design (like `shardguard`), so being uncalled by the server
  is correct. The real gap is that nothing can run it. It needs a small CLI **and** the
  `loop.turn.telemetry` log line enriched: it currently carries turn, cost, reasoning tokens and tool
  calls, but not `resident_tokens` / `considered_tokens` / `output_tokens`, which today exist only in
  the activity layer. Three of `TurnRecord`'s six fields are therefore not recoverable from the logs.
- **Voice s2s "fast mode"** (`realtime.go` + `bridge.go` + `bridge_provider.go`) is a complete, tested,
  unwired feature, **not** superseded dead code as an earlier note in this file claimed. Production runs
  the text-intermediate pipeline via `voice.NewVoiceSession`, which the code itself calls "the default
  because it is maximally governable". Enabling s2s is a **governance decision, not a wiring task**: the
  code is explicit that s2s trades mid-utterance spoken-PII tokenisation for latency. It should be wired
  behind an explicit per-tenant opt-in that defaults OFF, and that call belongs to the owner.

---

## Building the remaining gaps, not just wiring them (2026-07-23)

The previous pass wired what was wireable and deliberately stopped at four items that needed real
building. This pass built all four. Everything below is code-complete, wired, and green across the
full unit + `-tags=integration -p 1` suites against Postgres 16 in Docker.

### 1. Distilled-model quality circuit-breaker (the safety-critical one)

`DecideRollback` existed but nothing ran it, so a promoted distilled student that regressed after
promotion kept serving forever, quietly turning a cost saving into a quality regression.

Built `distill.RollbackMonitor` (+ the repository queries it needs: `ListPromoted`, `LivePassRate`,
`RollbackModel`) and wired it as a 15-minute sweep in the distillation block. It only ever moves a
model `promoted → rolled_back`, so the failure direction is SAFE (serve the frontier model again,
more expensive, correct). Guards, each with a test:

- **thin evidence** (partial window) never rolls back;
- a **freshly promoted** student is left alone (its window is dominated by the previous model's
  traffic, so judging it on that would attribute someone else's results to it);
- **per-pass cap** so a provider incident cannot roll back the whole fleet at once (10 regressed
  students is a provider problem, not 10 bad models);
- every error **fails safe** (holds the student), never rolls back on unknown data;
- reuses the SAME `eval_results` signal the promotion gate used, so promote and rollback are comparable
  and cannot flap.

Metrics `distill_rollback_total{outcome}` + `distill_promoted_models`; alerts `DistilledModelRolledBack`,
`DistilledModelRollbackWave`, `DistillRollbackMonitorFailing`. 8 unit tests.

### 2. loopeval is now runnable end to end

`internal/loopeval` is a pure offline scorer, correctly uncalled by the server — but nothing could
FEED it, and three of `TurnRecord`'s six fields (resident/considered/output tokens) were not in the
telemetry log at all.

- Added `InputTokens` / `OutputTokens` (the provider's OWN usage, i.e. what the model actually saw)
  to `StreamLLMResult`, populated from `usage`, and added `resident_tokens` / `output_tokens` to the
  `loop.turn.telemetry` log line. Additive + omitempty, so existing workflow histories replay
  unchanged.
- Built `cmd/loopeval`: reads exported orchestrator logs, groups by run, scores, and prints a table or
  `-json` for CI baselines. Skips non-telemetry lines so raw pod logs pipe in without pre-filtering;
  exits non-zero when it finds nothing (an empty baseline is almost always a wrong input). 7 tests plus
  a real end-to-end run through the built binary.

### 3. Onboarding sandbox — the datasets that were the actual blocker

The sandbox service was unwired because there was no provisioner and no synthetic data. Built all of it:

- **Four synthetic datasets** (hr / finance / it / procurement), embedded in the binary so the sandbox
  never depends on a network call during someone's first five minutes. Every value invented,
  example.com contacts (RFC 2606), and each dataset deliberately includes a case a governed agent must
  REFUSE or ESCALATE (over-threshold invoice, privileged prod access, lapsed-compliance supplier),
  because a walkthrough where the agent approves everything teaches the wrong thing about a governance
  product.
- **`repository.PgSandboxProvisioner`**: one schema per sandbox, seeded in a single transaction (a
  half-seeded sandbox is worse than none), `SET LOCAL search_path` so the seed needs no qualification
  and cannot leak into public. A regex + `pgx.Identifier` guard on the schema name because DDL cannot
  be parameterised; an integration test fires real injection strings at it and confirms `public`
  survives.
- **`repository.SandboxRepository`** + persistence on the `Service` + a **TTL reaper** goroutine, so a
  crash cannot strand provisioned schemas (a silent disk leak). A reload returns the EXISTING sandbox
  rather than provisioning a second schema, and a failed bookkeeping write drops the orphaned schema
  rather than leaking it.
- Routes: `POST /v1/onboarding/sandbox`. 5 unit + 3 integration tests (every dataset provisions and
  tears down cleanly against real Postgres).
- Six new `router.Primary()` sites, all correctly caught by the `shardguard` lint and annotated
  `control-plane` (the sandbox is primary-resident by design); audit doc row 12 added.

### 4. Voice speech-to-speech "fast mode" behind a double gate

The s2s bridge (`realtime.go` + `bridge.go` + `bridge_provider.go`) was a complete, tested, unwired
feature. Correcting the earlier note again: it is NOT superseded dead code. Wired it behind TWO gates,
OFF unless both are open:

1. the deployment configures a realtime provider (`ORCHESTRATOR_VOICE_REALTIME_*`), AND
2. the individual call's SIGNED session token carries `FastMode`.

It is on the signed token deliberately: s2s trades mid-utterance spoken-PII tokenisation for latency,
so the decision is the server's at mint time, never a caller's to flip by editing a parameter. Built
`telephony_realtime.go` adapting the push-based telephony shape onto the bridge's pull-based
`CallerAudio` (buffered, copies frames, drops-not-blocks so a slow model turn can never stall the media
stream). An unreachable/misconfigured provider DEGRADES to the fully governed text pipeline rather than
dropping the call, so enabling s2s can never reduce availability. Tool calls still route through the
supervisor — the moat holds in fast mode. 5 tests on the gate + audio-adapter invariants.

### What did NOT need doing (verified, not assumed)

- **SDK parity:** none. All four are internal/ops or server-minted. Onboarding is a browser/console
  flow, not a client-SDK surface; s2s is opted into by the server, not the SDK. Confirmed neither
  `actrone-ts` nor `actrone-py` has (or should have) an onboarding/s2s surface.
- **No SDK or FE source was touched**, so those trees are unchanged.

### One honest flag, not a gap I silently closed

The Control Tower already has a SEPARATE, client-side onboarding model
(`frontend/.../lib/onboarding.ts`): persona + checklist stored on tier metadata, completion derived
from real product signals. The backend flow I wired (the EMAOP §6.3/6.4 state machine + sandbox) is a
different model. The backend endpoints are complete and correct, but whether the console should ADOPT
them over its existing persona system is a product decision, not a wiring task, and building a second
FE onboarding UI would conflict with the first. I did not do that; it needs a product call on which
onboarding model wins.

### Verification

`go build ./...` OK, `gofmt` clean for touched files, `go vet` clean, **full unit suite green**
(including `shardguard`), and **`go test -tags=integration -p 1 ./...` fully green** against Postgres 16
in Docker plus Temporal dev servers. All changes remain **uncommitted**.

## Native SDK & A2A Compliance Plan (`docs/Actrone_Native_SDK_And_A2A_Compliance_Plan.md`)

Implementing the plan phase by phase (Phase 0 → Phase 9), each prod-grade per CLAUDE.md → tests green
→ marked ✅ → next. All uncommitted (owner commits).

### Phase 0 — A2A wire-type diff + multi-tenancy verification ✅ COMPLETE (investigation only, no code)

Exit criterion was *"a precise, written list of exactly what's missing, not an estimate."* Done by
reading the canonical spec source (`a2aproject/A2A@main:specification/a2a.proto`, the normative proto —
`specification/json/a2a.json` is a non-committed build artifact) plus `docs/specification.md` §5.5,
§8.3, §8.4, §9, and diffing field by field against `internal/a2a/spec.go`. Findings:

**1. The v1.0 diff is not a field patch — it is a second wire format.** The plan's §3.2 hoped `Part`'s
existing `Kind` discriminator "may already be close to where 1.0 wants implementations to be." It is
not. Confirmed breaking deltas, every one of which Actrone is on the v0.3 side of:

| Area | v0.3 (what Actrone has) | v1.0 (normative) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `AgentCard.url` / `protocolVersion` | top-level scalars | **removed**; replaced by `supportedInterfaces[]` of `AgentInterface{url, protocolBinding, tenant, protocolVersion}` |
| `AgentCard.signatures` | absent | `AgentCardSignature[]` (`protected`, `signature`, `header`) |
| `AgentCard.security` | `[]map[string][]string` | `securityRequirements[]` of `SecurityRequirement{schemes: map<string,StringList>}` |
| `AgentCard.iconUrl` / `documentationUrl` | absent | present (optional) |
| `AgentCapabilities` | `streaming`, `pushNotifications`, `stateTransitionHistory` | `streaming`, `pushNotifications`, `extensions[]`, `extendedAgentCard`; **`stateTransitionHistory` removed** |
| `AgentSkill` | no security | `+ securityRequirements[]`; `tags` is REQUIRED |
| `AgentProvider` | `organization` req, `url` opt | **both** REQUIRED |
| `Part` | `{kind, text, data, file:{name,mimeType,uri,bytes}}` | `oneof {text | raw | url | data}` + `metadata`, `filename`, `mediaType`; **`kind` removed**, discrimination by member presence; `FileContent` gone (`raw`=base64 bytes, `url`=URI) |
| `TaskState` | `"submitted"`, `"input-required"`… | `"TASK_STATE_SUBMITTED"`, `"TASK_STATE_INPUT_REQUIRED"`… (ProtoJSON SCREAMING_SNAKE); **`unknown` → `TASK_STATE_UNSPECIFIED`** |
| `Role` | `"user"` / `"agent"` | `"ROLE_USER"` / `"ROLE_AGENT"` |
| `Message` | no extensions/refs | `+ extensions[]`, `+ referenceTaskIds[]`, `+ metadata`; **`kind` discriminator removed** |
| `Task` / `Artifact` | `kind` discriminator on Task | `kind` removed; `+ metadata`; `Artifact + description`, `+ extensions[]` |
| Methods | `message/send`, `message/stream`, `tasks/get`, `tasks/cancel` | `SendMessage`, `SendStreamingMessage`, `GetTask`, `CancelTask`, **`ListTasks`**, `SubscribeToTask`, `GetExtendedAgentCard`, 4 × `*TaskPushNotificationConfig` |
| Send result | `Task` | `SendMessageResponse` = `oneof {task | message}` |
| Stream frame | raw `Task` in `result` | `StreamResponse` = `oneof {task | message | statusUpdate | artifactUpdate}`; `final` removed |
| Send config | `{blocking, acceptedOutputModes}` | `{acceptedOutputModes, taskPushNotificationConfig, historyLength, returnImmediately}` (**`blocking` inverted → `returnImmediately`**) |
| Version negotiation | none | `A2A-Version` request header (or query param); empty ⇒ 0.3; unsupported ⇒ `VersionNotSupportedError` **-32009** |
| Error codes | `-32001` only | `-32001`…`-32009` named set; `data` is an **array** of `{"@type": …}` objects (`google.rpc.ErrorInfo` with `reason` + `domain: "a2a-protocol.org"`) |
| Timestamps | RFC 3339 | ISO 8601 UTC **millisecond** precision |

*Conclusion:* a `ProtocolVersion` string flip would have been a false compliance claim, exactly as the
plan's §3.3 warned. The correct shape is a **dual-version surface** — which is what the spec itself
prescribes (§3.6.2: *"Agents CAN expose multiple interfaces for the same transport with different
versions under the same or different URLs"*), and matches the plan's own "advertise both during the
transition; don't force a hard cutover."

**2. Agent Card signing: confirmed absent, and the procedure is more specific than "sign the JSON".**
`internal/a2a/agentcard.go` signs nothing. The normative procedure (spec §8.4) is: strip default-valued
non-REQUIRED fields per proto field-presence rules → exclude `signatures` → canonicalize with **JCS
(RFC 8785)** → JWS-sign the `BASE64URL(protected) || '.' || BASE64URL(payload)` input → emit
`{protected, signature, header?}`. Protected header MUST carry `alg`, `kid` and SHOULD carry
`typ: "JOSE"`; MAY carry `jku`. So the build needs a real JCS implementation, not `json.Marshal`.

**3. Multi-tenancy: a REAL gap, but smaller and differently-shaped than the plan guessed.** The plan
asked whether "an external A2A caller can already discover and address one specific agent among a
tenant's many." Verified answer: **no, in two distinct ways.**
   - v1.0's mechanism is an **opaque routing identifier**: `AgentInterface.tenant` on the card, which
     the client MUST echo in the `tenant` field of *every* request message. Actrone has no such field
     anywhere on the wire — tenancy is resolved solely from the bearer token
     (`middleware.TenantIDFromContext`). That is secure but it is not the protocol's mechanism, and a
     compliant client that sets `tenant` gets it silently dropped.
   - `WellKnownCard` serves one static `PlatformCard()` (`CompileCard(base, nil, {})` — zero skills),
     and `CompileCardForCaller` unions *all* of a tenant's enabled profiles into a **single** card with
     skills namespaced `"{agentName}/{skillID}"`. So a caller can *invoke* a specific agent (the
     namespaced skill id is the address, read at `HandleSend` from `params.Metadata["skill"]`), but
     cannot **discover or fetch a card for** one specific agent. Per-agent addressability exists;
     per-agent *discovery* does not.

**4. Parity-plan dependencies: verified ABSENT, not assumed.** `grep`ed the backend directly —
`NodeGuard` has no definition anywhere (`agentgraph.NodeKind` is exactly `tool|llm|decision|end`), and
there is no `evaldataset`/`topicrails`/`promptvariants` package; `internal/eval` exposes only
`GET /v1/evals/agents/{agentId}/summary`. Consequences carried forward honestly rather than papered
over:
   - **§2.3's "guard nodes from day one" is blocked.** The SDK graph builder mirrors the four kinds
     that actually exist. Emitting a `guard` node the server would reject is a worse outcome than not
     offering it, and inventing a client-side `guard` would violate the plan's own "the Go
     `Validate()` is the single source of truth."
   - **Phase 6 (`agent.test()`) is blocked on the parity plan's Parts C/E**, exactly as the plan
     predicted. Recorded as a blocker at Phase 6 below, not silently worked around.

**5. Confirmed correct today, no work needed:** the well-known path is already `/.well-known/agent-card.json`
(1.0 naming); JSON-RPC 2.0 envelopes, SSE streaming, webhook push, `SignedPartStore`, the delegation-verified
registry, and the governance layer (vaulted credentials, SSRF screening, poisoning scans, spend caps,
hop audit) are real and stay unchanged under both versions.

### Phase 1 — Agent Card signing + dual-version interface (Part B) ✅ COMPLETE

Exit criterion was *"an external A2A 1.0 reference client verifies a signed Actrone Agent
Card successfully."* Met, and verified by actually running `a2aproject/a2a-sdk` v1.1.2 against
the real handler (see the interop harness below), not by a self-written mock.

Built, all in `backend/orchestrator/internal/a2a/`:

- **`jcs.go` — RFC 8785 (JCS) canonicalization.** Written in-repo rather than taken as a
  dependency: RFC 8785 is a frozen spec with published, exhaustive test vectors, so the whole
  surface is verifiable, and a signing-path primitive is not somewhere to add a 29-star,
  three-years-untouched module whose correctness we would have to take on faith. `jcs_test.go`
  runs the **official `cyberphone/json-canonicalization` vectors** (vendored under
  `testdata/jcs/`) — arrays, french, structures, unicode, values, weird — all green, plus
  idempotency, duplicate-key rejection, and the ES6 number-format boundaries. Two things the
  obvious implementation gets wrong and this one does not: keys sort by **UTF-16 code unit**
  (a supplementary-plane key sorts BELOW a high-BMP one, the opposite of its UTF-8 byte
  order), and duplicate member names are **rejected** rather than last-wins (a permissive
  parser lets a signer canonicalize one value and a verifier another, which is a signature
  bypass).
- **`specv1.go` — the complete A2A 1.0 wire model.** v0.3's types in `spec.go` are untouched
  and still serve the v0.3 interface; 1.0 is a second, parallel set. Covers every delta from
  the Phase-0 table: `supportedInterfaces`, `signatures`, the unified `Part` (no `kind`),
  ProtoJSON SCREAMING_SNAKE enums, `SendMessageResponse`/`StreamResponse` oneofs, `ListTasks`,
  and the `-32001`…`-32009` named error set with typed `google.rpc.ErrorInfo` details.
- **`cardsign.go` — spec-compliant JWS.** `alg: EdDSA` (RFC 8037) over Actrone's existing
  Ed25519 key idiom, but the **wire output is a plain RFC 7515 JWS**, so a standard JOSE
  library verifies an Actrone card with zero Actrone-specific code — asserted by
  `TestSignCard_VerifiesWithIndependentJWS`, which rebuilds the signing input from the spec's
  own recipe and checks it with a bare `ed25519.Verify`. Verification reads the RECEIVED bytes
  (never a struct round trip), so a member the struct does not model is still inside the
  signature; `alg: none` and every HS* algorithm are refused by allowlist.
- **`cardv1.go` — the 1.0 card compiler plus the transition-period hybrid card.** The hybrid
  carries both vocabularies in one served document, which is the migration path 1.0 itself
  prescribes. Skills are sorted: the card is signed, so a set-ordered skill list would make two
  requests describing the identical agent produce different bytes.
- **`version.go` + handler wiring** — `A2A-Version` negotiation (spec §3.6) on the SAME
  endpoint the card advertises. An empty header means 0.3, so **every integration that exists
  today is unaffected** (`TestRPC_NoVersionHeaderStaysV03`); an unsupported version gets the
  spec's own `VersionNotSupportedError` (-32009) rather than a silent downgrade.
- Config `ORCHESTRATOR_MCP_A2A_CARD_SIGNING_KEY_SEED` / `_ID` / `_JWKS_URL`, wired in
  `main.go`. Unset means unsigned cards (spec-legal, and today's behaviour);
  **present-but-malformed is fatal**, never a silent downgrade — an operator who provisions a
  signing key expects signatures.

**Two real defects the interop run caught that no amount of spec-reading would have:**

1. **`SecurityScheme` is a oneof WRAPPER in 1.0**, not v0.3's inline
   `{"type":"http","scheme":"bearer"}`. The correct shape is
   `{"httpAuthSecurityScheme":{"scheme":"bearer"}}`. Now modelled properly (`SecuritySchemeV1`
   plus the five scheme messages). This was invisible to a permissive JSON reader but changed
   the signed payload, so a reference client both misread the scheme and rejected the signature.
2. **The spec and the reference SDK disagree on what bytes a signature covers.** Spec §8.4.1
   keeps REQUIRED fields even when empty (its own worked example emits `"description":""` and
   `"skills":[]`); the SDK's `a2a/utils/signing.py` recursively deletes every empty string,
   list, and object first, which also prunes `securityRequirements` away entirely once the
   empty scope `list` inside it collapses its parents. Those are different byte strings, so a
   spec-literal card does not verify in the reference client and vice versa. **Resolution: sign
   the card TWICE** (`cardsign_compat.go`), which §8.4.3 explicitly sanctions — multiple
   signatures MAY be present, and a verifier succeeds on any one — so the card verifies under
   both readings with no guess about which is authoritative. Verification accepts either
   canonicalization too, so Actrone can verify a foreign card whichever way it was signed. When
   upstream converges, the extra signature simply becomes redundant.

**The interop gate** (`internal/handler/http/a2a_interop_test.go`, `//go:build interop`, driver
at `internal/a2a/interop/interop_client.py`): stands the real routes on a live listener and
drives them with `a2a-sdk[signing]` — its card resolver, its transport selection from
`supportedInterfaces`, **its own `create_signature_verifier`**, its protobuf types, its enum
names. Green end to end: discovery, signature verification, per-agent addressing, SendMessage,
GetTask, ListTasks, streaming, cancel, and not-found. Run with
`A2A_INTEROP_PYTHON=<venv>/bin/python go test -tags=interop ./internal/handler/http/ -run Interop`.
Build-tagged so the unit suite never depends on a Python environment.

**`ProtocolVersion` deliberately still reads `"0.3.0"`** for the v0.3 interface. It was never
flipped, exactly as the plan's §3.3 demanded: `ProtocolVersionV1 = "1.0"` is advertised on the
1.0 interface, and that claim is now backed by a passing external conformance run rather than
by a changed string.

### Phase 2 — Multi-tenancy: many agents behind one endpoint ✅ COMPLETE

Phase 0 confirmed the gap was real (no `tenant` field anywhere on the wire; one aggregate card
per tenant; per-agent *invocation* possible via namespaced skill ids, but no per-agent
*discovery*). Exit criterion was *"an external caller discovers and addresses two different
agents belonging to the same tenant through the same A2A endpoint, each with its own correct
card."* Met, and it is one of the assertions in the reference-client run above.

- **`TenantRoute`** implements 1.0's opaque routing identifier (`AgentInterface.tenant`, echoed
  by the client in every request). An empty value is a valid tenant-wide route, so pre-1.0
  behaviour is unchanged; a value the server never issued is an **error, not a widening** to
  tenant-wide, which would be an authorisation surprise.
- **`CompileCardV1ForCaller(..., route)`** serves a per-agent card that names THAT agent, with
  its own version, description, and only its skills. An unknown agent is not-found, never an
  empty card that looks valid.
- **`RouteScope.PermitsSkill`** makes the identifier load-bearing rather than decorative:
  addressing `agent:billing` cannot reach `support/triage`, asserted both at the service layer
  and over HTTP, including that the refused call never executed.
- **`GET /v1/a2a/agents`** enumerates the addressable agents with their routing identifiers —
  the discovery half, without which a caller would have to already know a namespaced skill id.
- Also closed while here: **`TaskNotCancelableError` (-32002)**. Cancelling an already-terminal
  task silently returned the completed task, so a client could not tell "cancelled" from
  "finished before your cancel landed". The 1.0 path now reports the protocol's own error; the
  **v0.3 path is deliberately left as it was**, since existing integrations were written
  against it and the whole premise is that adding 1.0 changes nothing for a 0.3 caller.

### Phase 3 — The simple authoring path + the graph builder (Part A §2.2-§2.3) ✅ COMPLETE

Exit criterion was *"a developer builds a native agent entirely in Python or TypeScript
through either path, no hand-typed dict, no Studio."* Met in both SDKs, and the manifest they
produce is verified against the **real kernel parser**, not against the SDKs' own idea of it.

`register_agent(agent_file: dict[str, Any])` / `registerAgent(agentFile: Record<string,
unknown>)` are unchanged — this is purely an SDK-side ergonomics layer over an untouched wire
contract, exactly as the plan required. No backend API change.

**The simple path is the default, and it is genuinely sufficient.** Four things get a working
agent — a name, an owner, instructions, a model:

```python
spec = (define_agent("invoice-triage", owner="finance@acme.com")
        .instructions("Triage inbound invoices. Escalate anything over $10,000.")
        .model("claude-opus-5", fallback=["claude-sonnet-5"])
        .tool("connector.netsuite.read", daily_budget_usd=5.0)
        .build())
await client.register_agent(spec.to_dict())
```

That is asserted, not asserted-in-prose: `TestSDKMinimalManifest_IsAcceptedByTheParser` runs
exactly what the simple path emits through `agent.ParseJSON` and checks the kernel fills in its
own defaults (max_tokens 4096, relevance 0.72, 30-minute task cap, async audit). This is why
an unconfigured section is **omitted entirely** rather than emitted as zeros: a block of zeros
would overwrite those defaults with values the developer never chose.

**The graph is the opt-in power path** (`actrone.graph.Graph` / `Graph` in `@actrone/sdk`),
fluent and chainable, with `validate()` mirroring `agentgraph.Graph.Validate()` invariant for
invariant: entry declared and non-empty, at least one node, unique ids with a known kind, edges
referencing declared nodes, at least one terminal so the graph can halt, every node reachable
from the entry. Cycles are explicitly allowed. `next()` sorts its successors, mirroring the
kernel's deterministic traversal (a durable workflow only replays identically if ordering is
stable). Every violation is reported at once rather than the first, matching how the server
reports agent-file validation.

**§2.3's "guard nodes from day one" is NOT built, and the builder deliberately does not offer
them.** Phase 0 confirmed `NodeGuard` has no definition anywhere in the backend —
`agentgraph.NodeKind` is exactly `tool|llm|decision|end`. Emitting a `guard` node the server
would reject is a worse outcome than not offering it, and inventing a client-side one would
break the plan's own rule that the Go `Validate()` is the single source of truth. Both SDKs
pin the four real kinds as a closed type (a `Literal` / a union), so `guard` is a **type error
at authoring time**, and both suites assert that. When the parity plan's Part A lands, adding
the kind is a one-line change in three places.

### Phase 4 — Memory, cost, scheduling, output and coordination builders (§2.4-§2.5) ✅ COMPLETE

Sequenced ahead of eval-testing and voice deliberately, per §1's finding that
context/memory/cost discipline is the larger named pain point. Exit criterion was *"an agent's
memory, loop policy, token budget, schedule, output schema, and coordination are all
configurable through typed builders, none requiring a hand-typed manifest field."* Met.

Shipped in `actrone-py/src/actrone/specs.py` and `actrone-ts/src/specs.ts`, with identical
field names, vocabularies and bounds:

| Section | What it exposes |
| --- | --- |
| `Memory` | session TTL, long-term on/off, retrieval budget, relevance threshold, auto-summarise + turn trigger |
| `LoopPolicy` | max turns, per-phase `thinking` + `model_tier`, `Compaction` (trigger / turn threshold / never-compact classes), reflection budget, tool-advertise strategy, cost SLO |
| `Limits` | hard daily/task cost, task duration, tool-call ceiling |
| `TokenBudget` | monthly ceiling + `pause`/`notify`/`hard_stop` |
| `Schedule` | trigger mode, cron or interval, task prompt, timezone, active-day/time window, overlap, run timeout, catch-up, auto-pause threshold |
| `OutputSchema` | name, JSON Schema, `off`/`strict`/`json`, bounded repair attempts |
| `Coordination` | spawn permission, allowed sub-agents, concurrency ceiling, shared memory, advertised capabilities, cross-tenant opt-in |
| `Safety`, `Governance` | output filter + PII detection; policy, audit mode, purpose limitation, MCP result governance |

Every enum is a closed type, so a typo is caught at authoring time rather than as a 400 after a
round trip. Bounds mirror the kernel's `validate` tags (`relevance_threshold` in the open
interval (0,1); `max_repair_attempts` 0-2 because each repair is a billable model call;
`every_seconds` ≥ 60). Local validators mirror the kernel's own rules where a field's meaning
depends on another: a scheduled agent needs a cadence *and* a task prompt and cannot set both
cron and interval; a cadence without `trigger_mode="scheduled"` is a **silent no-op
server-side**, so it is rejected locally instead; an enforced output contract needs both a name
and a schema; a compaction `turn_threshold` is meaningless without the `turn_count` trigger.

**Progressive disclosure is asserted as a test, not stated as a design note** (§2.2, and the
plan's §5 testing table): every section is parametrised over `to_dict() == {}` with zero
configuration, and a separate test walks all eleven optional sections confirming each is absent
from the manifest when untouched.

Two Python-specific details worth recording: `schema` collides with a pydantic `BaseModel`
attribute, so the field is `json_schema` with `alias="schema"` and `populate_by_name`, keeping
the wire name exact while either spelling constructs it; and `Graph.validate()` shadows
pydantic's deprecated `BaseModel.validate` classmethod, kept deliberately (with a justifying
`# UNSAFE:` comment per CLAUDE.md §9.3) because cross-language API parity with the Go
`Validate()` and the TypeScript `validate()` is a stated requirement, and pydantic's internals
call `model_validate`, never `validate`.

### Phase 3/4 verification — one manifest shape pinned in three languages

The parity requirement (§2.14) is enforced by a **single shared fixture**,
`actrone-ts/test/fixtures/parity-agent.json`, exercising every section including a branching,
cycling graph. Three suites assert against that one artefact:

1. **TypeScript** (`test/parity.test.ts`) builds it through the public API and compares.
2. **Python** (`tests/unit/test_parity.py`) does the same, reading the fixture from the sibling
   `actrone-ts/` checkout. It is stored once rather than copied into both repos on purpose: two
   copies of a contract are two things to forget to update, and a drifted duplicate would let
   the SDKs diverge while both suites stayed green. When the sibling checkout is absent the
   test **skips with a message naming the missing path**, rather than passing vacuously.
3. **Go** (`internal/agent/sdk_manifest_test.go`) vendors the same fixture and runs it through
   the real `agent.ParseJSON`. Cross-SDK agreement is worth nothing if the shape they agree on
   is one the kernel rejects, so the authority the SDK-side `validate()` mirrors gets the final
   say. It also checks the kernel's `Graph.Next()` agrees with the SDKs' `next()` mirror on the
   same graph.

The builders' `build()`/`toJSON()` also accept `validate=False` for deliberately inspecting an
incomplete definition, but validation is on by default — catching a bad manifest locally rather
than server-side is the reason the builders exist.

**Gates green:** `mypy --strict` clean (60 files), `ruff` clean on every new file, **334 Python
tests** passing; `tsc --noEmit` clean, **242 TypeScript tests** passing; the Go suite green
including the two new parser-contract tests. (Two pre-existing `ruff` E501s in
`harness/frameworks/{microsoft_agent_framework,semantic_kernel}.py` and some pre-existing
`gofmt` debt in files this work does not touch are left alone — they predate this plan.)

### Phase 5 — Voice, including outbound calling (Part A §2.6) ✅ COMPLETE

Exit criterion was *"a typed SDK call constructs both an inbound voice session and an outbound
consent-gated, spend-capped calling agent."* Met in both SDKs
(`actrone-py/src/actrone/voice_spec.py`, `actrone-ts/src/voice-spec.ts`).

Voice is a separate typed surface, not an extension of the graph builder, because it genuinely
is one in the kernel: it runs as `VoiceSessionWorkflow`, its own workflow type, not as a node
inside `agentgraph.Graph`.

**`VoiceCapabilities`** — the seven manifest flags `voice.EnforceCapability` accepts
(`make_voice_calls`, `answer_inbound_calls`, `chair_meetings`, `ivr_navigate`,
`live_translate`, `voicemail_drop`, `voice_approvals`), granted through
`.with_voice(...)` / `.withVoice(...)` into `spec.capabilities`. They stay seven separate
flags rather than one "voice" switch because they carry genuinely different risk: answering a
call the customer placed is not the same act as dialling one, and navigating someone else's
phone tree is not the same as either. Only granted flags are serialised — a capability the
manifest omits is denied anyway, so writing `false` would add noise without adding a
guarantee — and the order is fixed so a manifest cannot change shape because a map iterated
differently.

**`VoiceSession`** — inbound/interactive construction across the three transports the platform
actually runs (telephony/Twilio, meeting bots, browser/LiveKit). `consent_recorded` is
required with **no default**: the kernel refuses a session without it, and a field that quietly
defaulted to `true` would be a compliance bug wearing a convenience mask.

**`OutboundCall`** — the one place this SDK is deliberately stricter than everything else,
because an outbound call is a real phone ringing at a real person under DNC lists,
calling-window rules and marketing-consent law. The plan's §5 requires that the builder
*"cannot construct a call without the consent and spend-gate fields present, asserted as a
compile-time or immediate-validation failure, not a runtime surprise."* Both gates are
**required**, not optional:

- `consent_basis` / `consentBasis` has no default, because there is no safe default: a
  marketing call placed under an assumed "transactional" basis is a legal problem, not a
  misconfiguration.
- `spend_acknowledged` / `spendAcknowledged` is an explicit acknowledgement that the call
  spends real money against the tenant's voice budget and will be refused once the ceiling is
  reached. In TypeScript it is typed as the **literal `true`**, so omitting or negating it is
  a compile-time error; `buildOutboundCall` re-checks at runtime for the untyped caller (plain
  JS, or a value parsed from config) for whom the type is not a gate at all. In Python a
  model validator rejects a missing or false value at construction.

E.164 is validated locally on both the destination and the caller id, mirroring the kernel's
own boundary check, so a malformed number is an immediate local error rather than a 4xx round
trip. `spend_acknowledged` is deliberately **never sent on the wire**: it is an authoring-time
contract with the developer, and the ceiling itself is enforced server-side from tenant
configuration, which a client must not be able to influence — sending it would imply a control
it is not.

Docstrings state the retry semantics the SDK-locations memory already flags as a real
foot-gun: pass the **same** idempotency key on every retry of one logical call, because a
fresh key per attempt defeats deduplication and can dial a person repeatedly.

One deliberate reuse rather than a redeclaration: the TypeScript `ConsentBasis` already existed
in `types/voice.ts` for the runtime voice client, so `voice-spec.ts` re-exports it instead of
defining a second copy of the same vocabulary — two definitions of one contract is exactly the
drift this codebase's contract-first discipline exists to prevent.

**Gates green:** `mypy --strict` clean (61 files), `ruff` clean, **361 Python tests** (27 new);
`tsc --noEmit` clean, **24 new TypeScript tests**, full TS suite green.

### Phase 6 — Eval-testing integration (§2.7) ⛔ BLOCKED, not worked around

The plan gated this phase explicitly: *"Gated on the competitive parity plan's Part C/E shipping
first — `.test()` has nothing real to call until the eval-dataset generator and the extended
`loopeval` scoring exist"*, and its §8 requires the dependency be *"explicitly documented as a
blocker, not silently worked around."* It is blocked. Evidence, gathered by grep rather than
assumed:

| Needed by `agent.test(eval_dataset_id)` | Status |
| --- | --- |
| An eval-dataset generator (parity plan Part C) | **Absent.** No `evaldataset` package, no table, no endpoint anywhere in the backend. |
| Extended `loopeval` governance-accuracy scoring (Part E) | **Absent.** `internal/loopeval` is the offline scorer plus `cmd/loopeval`; it has no HTTP surface and nothing to score a dataset against. |
| An endpoint to run a dataset against an agent | **Absent.** `internal/eval` exposes exactly one route: `GET /v1/evals/agents/{agentId}/summary`. |

What DOES exist is already wired in both SDKs: `client.get_eval_summary(agent_id)` /
`client.getEvalSummary(agentId)` reads an agent's aggregate governed-eval quality over a
window. That is a real, useful surface, and it is not `.test()`.

**Nothing was shipped for this phase**, deliberately. A `.test()` that posted to a
non-existent endpoint would be the scaffolding CLAUDE.md §0.1 forbids, and — worse for this
plan specifically — it would make the SDK advertise a capability that cannot work, which is
the exact failure mode Part B spent Phase 0 refusing to commit for A2A. When the parity plan's
Parts C and E land, `.test()` is a thin ergonomics layer over them and belongs in this file
under the same heading.

> **Superseded (2026-09-09).** See "Closing the 'deliberately not built' list" below.

### Phase 7 (partial) — Replay / time-travel debugging (§2.9) ✅ COMPLETE

Exit criterion for this item: *"`agent.replay()` works against a real completed task."* Met, on
a real endpoint, in both SDKs (`actrone-py/src/actrone/replay.py`, `actrone-ts/src/replay.ts`).

The recording already existed, which is why the module is thin: every turn seals a six-step
reasoning trace (trigger → context → tokenisation → inference → evaluation → action) onto the
audit spine, served by `GET /v1/tasks/{taskID}/reasoning-trace` with the spine's HMAC over it.
Neither SDK wrapped it before; both do now, via `client.get_task_trace()` /
`client.getTaskTrace()` and the `client.replay` sub-client.

**The honesty boundary is the design**, and it is enforced by the API shape rather than a
comment:

- `replay.inspect(task_id)` returns the **whole** sealed trace, never a client-side slice — the
  HMAC covers all six steps, so a truncated trace could not be verified.
- `replay.steps(task_id, from_step=...)` fetches and narrows in one call. This is the actual
  time-travel: pick any recorded step and see everything from there.
- `replay.rerun(task_id, edited_input=..., commit=False)` starts a **new** run from the
  beginning, as a shadow fork that commits nothing unless asked. It wraps the existing
  `POST /v1/tasks/{taskID}/replay-edit`.

**Re-running from an arbitrary step N is deliberately NOT offered.** The plan's §2.9 phrasing
("re-runs or inspects a task from any recorded step") would allow a `from_step` on the re-run,
but no endpoint resumes mid-run, and an API that accepted the parameter while silently starting
over would be a worse lie than an absent feature. So `from_step` narrows **inspection only**,
`rerun` has no such parameter, and both suites assert that difference by signature inspection.

> **Superseded (2026-09-09).** See "Closing the 'deliberately not built' list" below.

An early draft of `inspect()` accepted `from_step` and ignored it; that dead parameter was
removed rather than documented, since a parameter that is accepted and does nothing is a bug
the docstring cannot fix. Every step model is `extra="allow"` / `.passthrough()`: the trace is
an audit record, so a field added server-side must reach the caller rather than be dropped by
an older SDK. A task with no trace yet reports `found=False` rather than raising — "not
recorded yet" is a normal state for a run before its first sealed turn.

### Phase 8 — Three deployment targets, one authoring surface (§2.13) ✅ COMPLETE

Exit criterion was *"the same agent definition deploys successfully to managed cloud, to a
self-hosted instance, and runs standalone under `target='connected'`, with the documented
governance tradeoff holding in the third case."* Met.

The mapping onto the existing manifest is real, not invented: `target` compiles to
`spec.runtime.build_mode`, the field the kernel already uses to route a task to the native
durable loop or to a connected framework loop.

| Target | `build_mode` | Durable loop |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `managed_cloud` (default) | `native` (left unstated) | yes |
| `self_hosted` | `native` (left unstated) | yes |
| `connected` | `byof_connected` | **no** |

Two decisions worth recording:

1. **`managed_cloud` and `self_hosted` serialise to the byte-identical manifest**, asserted in
   both suites. Which deployment the SDK talks to is client configuration (a base URL), not
   something the agent declares about itself; encoding the hosting choice into the manifest
   would make an agent non-portable between the two for no benefit. `native` is also the
   kernel's own default, so it is omitted entirely rather than written out — otherwise two
   identical agents would serialise differently depending on how they were authored.
2. **The tradeoff is a runtime value, not a doc comment.** §2.13's named risk is that
   `connected` gets misread as "the same guarantees as native, just self-hosted", so
   `deployment_tradeoff()` / `deploymentTradeoff()` returns the plain statement — *your*
   process runs the loop, no Temporal-backed crash-resume, no kernel-level capability ceiling
   on the loop itself, but every governed action still routes through the gateway and is
   audited — and `is_durable` / `isDurable` exposes the one guarantee that actually differs.
   The tests assert the connected text says what is lost AND what is retained.

`for_target()` / `forTarget()` rebinds an existing definition without rebuilding it: one
authoring surface, three destinations, with the tests confirming only the runtime block
differs. A bug caught while testing: Python's `model_copy(update=…)` does not re-validate, so
an invalid target survived construction and would have surfaced as a lookup error in
`build_mode`, far from the line that caused it; `for_target` now checks explicitly.

**Gates green:** `mypy --strict` clean (62 files), `ruff` clean on all new files, **382 Python
tests**; `tsc --noEmit` clean, **284 TypeScript tests** across 23 files.

### Phase 7 (remainder) — Durability demo, local dev view, shadow-agent audit ✅ COMPLETE

**§2.8 — the durability demo.** `actrone-py/examples/durability.py` and
`actrone-ts/examples/durability.ts`: submit a deliberately multi-step task, print the task id,
and invite the reader to kill the script and re-run it with that id. The task keeps going.

The example states the distinction in the terms the plan uses, because "durable" is used
loosely: checkpointing (LangGraph, CrewAI, Microsoft Agent Framework, Google ADK, Strands)
restarts from the last saved point, redoing the work in between and repeating any side effect
in that window; durable execution replays the recorded event history, so a completed tool call
is replayed from its result rather than re-executed, and the task resumes at the exact step it
died at. It also makes a second point the plan did not: **the crash-resume does not depend on
the demo process at all** — the loop runs in the kernel as a Temporal workflow, so the script
is only a client, and killing it proves the run survives losing both its client and a worker.
It polls rather than streams deliberately: a stream would make the connection look
load-bearing, which is precisely what the demo is disproving. It finishes by printing the
sealed trace, which is the receipt showing each step ran once.

Both typecheck against the live SDK (`mypy --strict`; `npm run typecheck:examples`).

**§2.10 — `actrone dev`.** Watches one task and prints each recorded step as the kernel seals
it, so a developer iterating sees what the agent actually did without leaving the terminal.
Stopping the watch does not affect the task, and the command says so.

It deliberately does **not** build a second trace visualisation: §2.10's own instruction is to
reuse Control Tower's `TraceViewer` "rather than building a second visualisation surface", so
`--open` prints the Control Tower URL for the task instead of reimplementing that view in the
terminal. The URL is printed rather than launched — a CLI that opens a browser behind your
back is a surprise over SSH and in CI, and the URL is what a developer wants to paste anyway.
The step renderer is shared with `tasks trace`: one way to display a trace, two entry points.

**§2.12 — shadow-agent detection: `actrone agents audit [path]`.** Scans a codebase for model
calls that reach a provider directly instead of through the governed gateway — uncapped,
unaudited, and invisible to every server-side view. The scan is **local** because that is
where the evidence is: a server cannot report an agent it was never told about.

Design choices worth recording:
- It matches the **endpoint**, not the import. Importing a provider SDK is not a bypass;
  pointing it at the provider is. That is what keeps the false-positive rate low enough for the
  report to be read rather than ignored. A client pointed at the Actrone gateway is correctly
  clean, asserted as a test.
- Findings are labelled **signals, not verdicts**, in the report itself: a deliberate direct
  call in a test fixture is indistinguishable from a real bypass at this level, and claiming
  otherwise trains people to ignore the output.
- **Secrets are redacted before display, endpoints are not.** A hardcoded provider key is a
  finding, and the report gets pasted into tickets, so echoing the key back would spread the
  exact secret the finding exists to flag. A first pass redacted the matched URL too, which
  left a report saying a line was a problem while hiding the part that made it one; URLs are
  now kept, since for an endpoint finding the URL *is* the evidence.
- Vendored and generated trees (`node_modules`, `.venv`, `vendor`, `.next`, …) are pruned, the
  file-type filter skips prose and data, output is deterministically ordered so two runs diff
  cleanly, and a finding **exits non-zero** so CI can fail a pull request that adds one.

**Scope stated honestly, in the code and here:** this is the codebase half of §2.12. The
org-wide half — correlating gateway traffic against registered agents — needs the competitive
parity plan's Part D query tooling, which Phase 0 confirmed does not exist. Rather than
approximate it with a weaker signal and give it the same name, the command does the half it
can do completely and says so in its own package comment.

> **Superseded (2026-09-09).** See "Closing the 'deliberately not built' list" below.

### Phase 9 — CLI parity (Part C) ✅ COMPLETE for everything not parity-plan-gated

`actrone-cli/cmd/agents.go` had carried its own trigger condition since the first release:
*"Writes (register/update/delete) exist in the SDK but are intentionally left out of this CLI
release... add them when the agent-authoring UX is designed."* Phases 3-5 designed it, so the
writes landed.

**Shipped (§4.2):**

| Command | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| `agents create -f <manifest>` | Posts the manifest **bare**, as `POST /v1/agents` expects — asserted, since an `{"agent_file": …}` envelope would be silently wrong |
| `agents update <id> -f <manifest>` | |
| `agents delete <id> --confirm` | Refuses without `--confirm`: deletion is permanent and takes every version. Not an interactive prompt — the CLI runs unattended in CI, where a prompt either hangs or is auto-answered, and neither is a safeguard |
| `agents audit [path]` | §2.12, above |
| `tasks trace <id> [--from-step]` | §2.9's CLI mirror; step numbering reflects the true position, not a restart at 1 |
| `tasks replay <id> --input` | Shadow by default; `--commit` says `COMMITTING` loudly; auto-generates an idempotency key so a retried replay coalesces |
| `dev <task-id> [--open]` | §2.10, above |
| `a2a card <base-url>` | Fetches a remote well-known card and summarises interfaces, skills, and **decoded JWS protected headers** — exactly the interop verification Part B's §3.6 testing needs, without `curl \| jq \| base64 -d` |
| `a2a agents` | The tenant's A2A-addressable agents with their routing identifiers |
| `init <name> --lang python\|typescript` | Project scaffold |

Two security details in `a2a card`: it does **not** attach Actrone credentials, because it is a
GET against a third party's server and the point is to see what that endpoint publishes to
anyone (asserted); and it **refuses plaintext HTTP** for a non-loopback host, because the card
names the endpoint a caller will then trust, so fetching it over a tamperable channel defeats
reading it at all.

**`actrone init` produces a project that actually works**, which §5 requires and which was
verified by generating both scaffolds and compiling them against the real SDKs:
`mypy --strict` on the generated `agent.py`, and `tsc --strict` on the generated `agent.ts`
resolved against `actrone-ts/src`. That verification caught **two real bugs** that reading
would not have:

1. The Python scaffold called `client.wait_for_task(...)` and `ActroneClient(SDKConfig(...))`
   — neither exists. The real API is `ActroneClient(api_key=…)` and `run_agent()`, which
   submits and polls. It also passed `agent.id` (a `UUID`) where `run_agent` takes `str`.
2. The TypeScript scaffold would not compile because `AgentDefinition.toJSON()` returned an
   `interface`, and TypeScript gives implicit index signatures to object **type aliases** but
   not to interfaces — so the value was not assignable to `registerAgent`'s
   `Record<string, unknown>`. Fixed in the SDK by making `AgentFileJSON` a type alias, rather
   than making every caller write a cast to hand a function the exact value it was built to
   receive.

The scaffold is the **simple path only** — a name, an owner, instructions, a model, one tool —
and a test asserts it does *not* preload the graph, schedule, loop or output-schema builders:
a file pre-filled with the whole configuration surface would undo the progressive disclosure
the SDK was designed around. The README points to each opt-in builder instead. It refuses to
overwrite an existing file (there is no undo in a terminal), validates the project name against
the kernel's own agent-name rule, and offers **no Go option**, since the Go SDK was removed
from the family.

**Deliberately not built (§4.3):** an interactive agent-construction wizard. That would
duplicate either the typed SDK builders in a third language or EMAOP's conversational builder
in a terminal. The CLI's role stays "operate on and inspect what the SDK, Studio, or EMAOP
produced."

**Blocked, not skipped:** §4.2 also lists `topic-rails`, `eval-datasets`, `query`, and
`prompt-variants`. Every one is an operational command over a competitive-parity-plan surface
(Parts C, D, E) that Phase 0 verified does not exist — there is no endpoint for any of them.
They are a thin cobra layer over those endpoints once they land, and shipping commands that
call nothing would be scaffolding.

**Gates green:** `gofmt` clean, `go vet` clean, full CLI suite green (7 `init` tests, 15
write/trace/replay/a2a tests, 11 audit/dev tests, plus the pre-existing suite).

### Native SDK & A2A Compliance Plan — STATUS: 9 of 10 phases COMPLETE, 1 blocked upstream

Against the plan's own §8 definition of done:

| Definition-of-done item | Status |
| --- | --- |
| A developer builds and deploys a native agent entirely in Python or TypeScript, either path, no hand-typed dict, no Studio | ✅ Phases 3-4; verified against the real kernel parser |
| Memory, loop/cost policy, token budget, schedule, output schema, coordination and voice all typed, with working defaults | ✅ Phases 4-5; "works with zero configuration" asserted per section |
| `agent.test()` returns a real scored eval report | ⛔ **Blocked** on the competitive parity plan's Parts C/E (verified absent, not assumed) |
| `agent.replay()` returns a real completed task's recorded steps | ✅ Phase 7, on the real `reasoning-trace` endpoint |
| The same definition deploys to managed cloud, self-hosted, or `connected`, with the tradeoff documented and tested | ✅ Phase 8 |
| An external, independent A2A 1.0 client discovers Actrone's signed card, exchanges messages, streams, and cancels | ✅ Phases 1-2; green against `a2a-sdk` 1.1.2 |
| The multi-tenancy question resolved with evidence | ✅ Phase 0 found a real gap; Phase 2 closed it |
| Every CLI command in §4.2 works; `actrone init` produces a working scaffold | ✅ Phase 9 for everything not parity-plan-gated; both scaffolds compile against the real SDKs |
| SDK parity confirmed via contract tests; CI gates green | ✅ One shared fixture pinned in Python, TypeScript **and** Go |
| Phase 6's dependency documented as a blocker, not silently worked around | ✅ Above, with the grep evidence |

**What the plan predicted and what actually happened.** Phase 0 was investigation-only on the
plan's insistence, and it earned that: the plan hoped `Part`'s existing `kind` discriminator
"may already be close to where 1.0 wants implementations to be", and it is not — v1.0 is a
second wire format, not a field patch. Flipping `ProtocolVersion` would have been a false
compliance claim, exactly as §3.3 warned. `ProtocolVersion` still reads `"0.3.0"` today;
`ProtocolVersionV1` is advertised on a separate interface, and that claim is now backed by a
passing external conformance run.

**Three real defects that only running the reference client could find**, each recorded in its
phase above: `SecurityScheme` is a oneof wrapper in 1.0 rather than v0.3's inline object; the
spec and the reference SDK genuinely disagree about what bytes a card signature covers
(resolved by dual-signing, which §8.4.3 sanctions); and cancelling an already-terminal task
silently reported success instead of `TaskNotCancelableError`.

**Two more that only compiling the generated scaffold could find**: the Python scaffold called
three APIs that do not exist, and the TypeScript scaffold could not compile because
`AgentFileJSON` was an `interface` rather than a type alias.

**Four things deliberately NOT built, each with its reason stated in the code as well as here**,
because an SDK that advertises a capability it does not have is the failure mode this whole
plan exists to avoid:

1. **`agent.test()`** — no eval-dataset generator, no scoring endpoint (parity plan Parts C/E).
2. **`guard` graph nodes** — `agentgraph.NodeKind` has no `guard`; both SDKs make it a
   compile-time error rather than emit a node the server rejects.
3. **Re-running a task from an arbitrary step** — no endpoint resumes mid-run, so `from_step`
   narrows inspection only and `rerun` has no such parameter, in both SDKs and the CLI.
> **Superseded (2026-09-09).** All four were re-examined and built, except the
> `topic-rails` / `query` / `prompt-variants` CLI groups, whose server surfaces still do
> not exist. See "Closing the 'deliberately not built' list" below.

4. **The org-wide half of shadow-agent detection, and the `topic-rails` / `eval-datasets` /
   `query` / `prompt-variants` CLI groups** — every one is an operational command over a
   parity-plan surface that does not exist.

**Everything is uncommitted** (owner commits). Touched: `backend/orchestrator/internal/{a2a,
handler/http,agent,config}` + `cmd/orchestrator/main.go`; `actrone-py/src/actrone/{graph,specs,
agent,voice_spec,replay,client,__init__}.py` + tests + `examples/durability.py`;
`actrone-ts/src/{graph,specs,agent,voice-spec,replay,client,index}.ts` + tests +
`examples/durability.ts` + `test/fixtures/parity-agent.json`; `actrone-cli/cmd/*` +
`internal/client/{endpoints,types}.go`.
