## Closing the "deliberately not built" list (2026-09-08, follow-on)

The four items the plan left unbuilt were re-examined on the owner's instruction. Three of the
four turned out to be mine to fix; the reasons they were skipped differed, and one of them
exposed a claim I had shipped that was not true.

### The correction that started it

`internal/agentgraph`'s package comment has always said "the runtime compiles each node to a
crash-resumable, governed Temporal activity (the infra follow-on)". **That runtime did not
exist.** Verified by grep: `agentgraph` was referenced in exactly one non-test place — the
`Graph` field on `domain.AgentSpec`. `HasGraph()` had no caller. `Graph.Next()` had no caller
outside its own tests. A manifest's `spec.graph` was parsed, validated, stored, and then
ignored at run time.

I had repeated that claim in both SDKs' graph docstrings ("compiled server-side to
crash-resumable, governed Temporal activities") without verifying it. That was wrong, and the
fix was to make it true rather than soften the wording.

### G1 — Graph execution ✅ BUILT (`internal/workflow/graph_workflow.go`)

A durable walk over the declared graph, wired into `AgentTaskWorkflow` behind an
`agent-graph` version gate and `HasGraph()`. The version gate matters more than usual here:
graph manifests were accepted and stored for a long time before there was a runtime, so any
history recorded before this replays down the loop path it actually took.

**The distinction the graph exists for**, stated in the code: in the loop the MODEL decides
which tools to call and when to stop; in a graph the AUTHOR decides and the model fills in the
steps. That is only worth something if it is enforced, so a model request for a tool the graph
does not run next is **parked, not executed** — asserted by a test where the model asks for
`dangerous_write` alongside the approved `safe_read` and only `safe_read` runs. Without that,
the graph would be decorative and the model would still be driving.

Node semantics:
- **`llm`** — one governed model turn. `ref` optionally pins this step's model, so an author
  can route a cheap classification and an expensive synthesis through one graph without a Loop
  Policy. Reports `tool_requested` / `answered` as branchable conditions.
- **`tool`** — runs the tool named in `ref`. Arguments come from a parked model request for
  that tool when one exists, else the task input — the fallback is what makes a **tool-first**
  graph possible, since a flow that fetches before it reasons has no model turn to take
  arguments from yet. Synthetic call ids are deterministic (`graph-<node>-<visit>`): workflow
  code cannot use a random UUID without breaking replay, and the id must still be unique across
  visits so a cycle's repeated calls are distinguishable in the audit. A blocked or failed tool
  is a **branchable** outcome (`blocked` / `error` / `ok`), not an automatic abort, so a graph
  can compensate.
- **`decision`** — the model picks among the node's *declared* edge conditions. The prompt is
  built from a sorted, deduplicated choice list (deterministic ⇒ replay-safe), and an
  unrecognised answer falls through to the unconditional edge rather than being invented into a
  branch: the author owns the fork, the model only picks an arm.
- **`guard`** — see below.
- **`end`** — halt.

Everything else is shared with the loop deliberately — the same governed activities, the same
cost ceiling, the same operator pause/resume/cancel channel, the same audit. A graph is a
different control-flow shape over one governed substrate, not a second runtime with its own
guarantees to re-establish.

Halting is guaranteed by a **visit budget**, not by graph shape: cycles are legal, so
reachability of a terminal is not enough. The cost ceiling is checked after *every* node, not
only after model calls, so a graph whose expensive step is a tool stops at the same limit.

**A real bug caught by the tests:** the budget was computed as
`Loop.EffectiveMaxTurns(Limits.MaxToolCallsPerTask)`, but that helper clamps to a minimum of 1
— so an agent with no declared ceiling got a budget of **one node** and every graph halted
after its first step. Fixed: the default is the fallback, and declared limits only tighten it.

### NodeGuard ✅ BUILT — the item that was "not built" because it had nothing to run on

`guard` is now a real node kind in the kernel (`agentgraph.NodeGuard`) and in both SDKs.

Per-tool governance checks already fire wherever the model happens to call a tool. A guard
fires where the **author** says it must — before a write, before an irreversible step. That is
the difference between a control and a hope, and it is why the node needed the executor first:
adding `guard` to a model nothing executes would have been decoration on decoration.

Behaviour, all tested:
- Passing verdict ⇒ follows the `allowed` condition.
- `Escalated` and `Blocked` are kept **distinct**, not conflated: an escalation pauses for a
  human decision and can still proceed on approval; conflating them would either deny work a
  reviewer would have approved, or wave through a refusal.
- A refusal follows a declared `when: "blocked"` edge so an author can compensate or notify;
  with no such edge it **halts** the run, because continuing past a refused guard would make
  the node decorative.
- **An unreachable policy engine fails CLOSED.** Treating a governance outage as approval would
  turn an incident into an ungoverned run — the single worst failure mode this node exists to
  prevent.

### Resume-from-node ✅ BUILT — the item that was "not built" because no step was addressable

`runGraphFrom(..., startNode)` continues a walk from a named node. It is a separate parameter
rather than a mutated entry, so the manifest is never rewritten to record where a replay began:
the graph an agent declares must not depend on how it was last started.

This is what "resume from step N" could not mean before. The reasoning trace's six coarse steps
are an *audit* record, not addressable execution points; a graph's nodes are. The test asserts
the property that makes it a resume rather than a re-run: an expensive prefix node is **not**
re-executed.

### SDK parity for the above

Both SDKs now expose `guard` plus a `GUARD_BLOCKED` constant, with `ref` documented per kind
(tool name / model pin / guard reason). The node-kind lists grew from four to five and remain
closed sets — a builder that emitted a kind the kernel rejects would still be worse than one
that does not offer it. Both graph docstrings now describe execution accurately instead of
repeating the plan's aspiration.

**Gates:** `go build ./...`, `go vet`, `gofmt` clean; 18 new graph-executor tests plus the
existing workflow suite green; `mypy --strict` clean; `tsc --noEmit` clean; 19 graph tests in
each SDK.

One test-harness note recorded because it looks like a papered-over failure and is not:
Temporal's deadlock detector measures **real wall-clock** time, so a multi-node walk with
instantly-returning mocks trips the one-second default on a loaded machine even though nothing
blocks (`applyLoopControl` uses `ReceiveAsync` and never waits). The graph test env raises
`DeadlockDetectionTimeout` to a minute so the suite is deterministic rather than a function of
what else is running. A genuine deadlock still fails, just later.

### `agent.test()` + eval datasets ✅ BUILT: the item that was "not built" because scoring had no corpus

A dataset is a corpus of cases pinned to one agent (`migrations/00139_eval_datasets.sql`:
`eval_datasets`, `eval_dataset_cases`, plus the per-run columns on `eval_runs`). Running it
replays every case as a real task and scores two questions that are deliberately separate:

- **"Is the answer still right?"** scored by lexical overlap, not equality. An LLM's prose is
  not deterministic, and a suite that goes red on rewording is a suite people learn to ignore.
- **"Does governance still decide the same way?"** scored **exactly**. For a governance
  product this is the one that matters: a policy regression is a real defect, and unlike
  prose, a verdict genuinely is deterministic.

A case may assert either, both, or neither, and `governance_accuracy` returns *nothing* rather
than `0` or `1` when no case asserted a verdict. "Governance was not measured" reading as
"governance failed" would train people to ignore the number; reading as "governance is
perfect" is worse.

**Preview execution is set server-side, not by the caller** (`internal/service/eval_case_runner.go`).
A suite pointed at a production agent runs the same workflow, the same governance and the same
tools as live traffic, and commits nothing. That combination is what makes it safe to point at
production, which is exactly when you most want to.

Two things the tests caught that would have shipped as quiet wrongness:

- `ScoreOutput` was measuring **precision**, so an answer that was correct *and elaborated*
  scored lower than a terse one. It measures recall of the expected text now.
- `verdictFor` originally inferred a verdict from task status. It reads the sealed reasoning
  trace and returns **empty** when there is none, because a guessed verdict compared exactly
  against an asserted one is a false red or a false green, never a measurement.

SDK parity: `actrone-py/src/actrone/evals.py` and `actrone-ts/src/evals.ts`, 14 Python and 13
TypeScript tests.

### Org-wide shadow-agent detection ✅ BUILT: the half the CLI scan structurally could not see

`actrone agents audit <path>` scans source for calls that reach a provider directly. It cannot
see a caller whose source nobody handed you. `actrone agents audit --org` asks the server which
callers actually reached the gateway and whether each is a registered agent. Neither half
subsumes the other, and the CLI's long help now says so instead of pointing at a plan.

The signal is attribution. The gateway already accepted an optional `X-Actrone-Agent` header;
nothing recorded whether callers send it, so the question "what is hitting our gateway that we
cannot account for?" had no answer. `gateway_attribution_daily` (migration `00140`) is that
record, and three design choices are load-bearing:

- **A daily rollup, not a per-call log.** Every row is written on the inference hot path. A row
  per call would add a write to every model request and grow without bound, which is a real
  cost for an audit signal. A counter per (tenant, day, caller, env) answers the same question.
- **`agent_ref` is unconstrained TEXT, deliberately.** The two most interesting rows are
  precisely the ones a foreign key would reject: the empty string (a caller that sent no
  header) and a stale or malformed id (a caller claiming an agent that does not exist).
  Constraining the column would discard exactly the evidence the table exists to collect.
- **No prompts, no completions, no request content.** This is an attribution ledger, not a
  traffic log. Widening it into one would turn a counting problem into a content-retention
  liability.

Recording is an in-memory counter flushed every 30s, bounded at 10,000 buckets because
`agent_ref` is caller-controlled and an unbounded map would be a memory-growth lever driven by
a request header. Every failure mode drops counts rather than touching the request: making a
model call fail because a counter could not be written would trade a real capability for a
bookkeeping one.

**What it does not see, stated in the code, the CLI report and both SDKs:** only traffic that
*reached the gateway*, and an unattributed caller is not necessarily malicious. A notebook, a
one-off script and a real bypass look identical from here. Findings are evidence for a human.

A deployment without attribution enabled returns **501 with a reason**, not an empty report:
"nothing was measured" presented as "nothing was unaccounted for" is the one failure that would
make this actively harmful. The same distinction runs through the report itself, which is why
it carries `total_calls` as a denominator, and why the CLI prints "no gateway traffic recorded"
rather than "all attributed" when there is none.

SDK parity: `client.shadow_audit()` / `client.shadowAudit()`, with `unaccounted_share`
recomputed from the counts in both rather than read off the wire, so it can never disagree with
the numbers printed beside it.

**A real bug the fixtures caught:** all three new test suites asserted against a *nested*
`{"error": {...}}` envelope, while the server writes a flat `{"code", "message"}`. The Python
and Go clients fall back to the raw response body on a parse miss, so both suites passed for
the wrong reason and only TypeScript went red. Fixtures corrected to the shape the server
actually emits.

**Gates:** `go build ./...`, `go vet`, `gofmt` clean (the five files `gofmt -l` reports are
pre-existing CRLF, untouched here); 17 new `shadowaudit` tests; full CLI suite green including
9 new `--org` tests; `mypy --strict` clean with 407 Python unit tests passing (10 new); `tsc
--noEmit` clean with 309 TypeScript tests passing (11 new).

One pre-existing typecheck break fixed in passing: `test/evals.test.ts` built a `Report` from a
`Partial<Report>`, which cannot express a partial *wire* payload once the schema's defaults are
applied. It now parses a partial wire object, which is what the client does with a real
response, so the fixture cannot assert a shape the parser would never produce.

### `actrone evals` CLI group ✅ BUILT, and the three groups that stay unbuilt, with the reason

Item 4 of the "deliberately not built" list bundled the org-wide shadow audit with four CLI
groups. Building the eval-dataset surface above made one of them buildable, so it is built:
`actrone evals {list,get,create,generate,delete,run,report}`.

The two properties that make it worth having rather than decorative:

- **`run --wait` exits non-zero on a failing suite.** That is what makes it a CI gate rather
  than a report someone reads afterwards. A run that itself crashed exits non-zero too, with
  the crash, rather than a pass rate computed over cases that never executed.
- **"Governance was not measured" prints as exactly that**, never as 0% or 100%. The former
  reads as "governance failed" and the latter as "governance is perfect", when the truth is
  that no case asked the question. The same distinction the API and both SDKs make.

Preview execution stays server-side, and the CLI has no flag to turn it off. A flag that could
would make a test suite capable of committing writes, which is precisely the property that
lets someone point a suite at a production agent.

`create` validates the case file before the call: a case with no input is refused, because a
case that runs nothing would pad the pass rate with a result that measured nothing. `delete`
requires `--confirm`, because removing a suite discards written-down expectations and leaves
past reports scored against a corpus that no longer exists.

> **Superseded (2026-09-09).** All three were built, server surfaces included. See
> "Competitive Parity Plan, Parts A, D and E" below.

**`topic-rails`, `query` and `prompt-variants` stay unbuilt, and this is not the same kind of
decision as the other three.** Those three were skipped because a piece I could build was
missing; these are operational commands over server surfaces that genuinely do not exist. A CLI
group whose every subcommand returns 404 is worse than no group at all: it advertises a
capability, teaches people it is broken, and is exactly the failure this plan set out to avoid.
They become a half-day's work the moment their endpoints land.

**Gates:** `go build ./...`, `go vet`, `gofmt` clean; 15 new `evals` CLI tests; full CLI suite
green.

## Competitive Parity Plan, Parts A, D and E (2026-09-09)

Built on the owner's instruction to close the three CLI groups previously left unbuilt
(`topic-rails`, `query`, `prompt-variants`). They were not CLI work: each needed its server
surface built first, so this is Parts A, D and E of `Actrone_Competitive_Parity_Improvements_Plan.md`,
backend + SDK + CLI. The frontend surfaces those parts also describe are NOT built and are the
remaining gap.

### Part D: governed query tooling ✅ BUILT

`internal/govquery` compiles a structured grammar into parameterized SQL. The design follows from
one constraint: this is a query surface, so it is the most attractive injection target in the
codebase.

- **No SQL text ever comes from a client.** A caller sends (field, operator, value); every
  identifier reaching the SQL is looked up in a hand-reviewed allowlist and emitted from OUR
  string constant, every value becomes a placeholder, and the operator set is closed.
- **A field not on the allowlist is REJECTED, never dropped.** Dropping a filter silently widens
  a result set, and on governance data a wider set means rows nobody asked for.
- **Tenant scope is imposed by the compiler and is not expressible in the grammar.** That is the
  single most important property here, and the test that pins it is the one for an OR at the top
  of the caller's tree: the whole caller tree is ANDed beneath the tenant predicate, in its own
  parentheses, so a disjunction cannot reach around it.
- `tenant_id` is deliberately absent from every source's field list. Exposing it would invite a
  query that appears to scope itself while the real scoping happens elsewhere, and the first time
  those disagreed would be a cross-tenant read.

Two mechanisms make it more than a read API, both reusing what exists rather than duplicating it:
promoting a saved query into a standing `notify` rule, and sealing a result as evidence with the
same recomputable SHA-256 the compliance packages use.

The alert is **edge-triggered**, not level-triggered: a rule notifies when it STARTS matching and
stays quiet while the condition persists. A rule that re-alerted every sweep is one people mute,
and a muted alert is worse than none because it still looks like coverage.

Migration `00141`. 55 package tests, 17 handler tests, 30 CLI tests, 16 per SDK.

### Part E: governance-aware prompt-ops ✅ BUILT

Comparing prompts is an ACTION over existing infrastructure, not a new artifact type. A variant
run IS an eval run, executed as preview tasks with the variant's prompt substituted; promoting a
winner calls the agent's ordinary update path, which snapshots a version like any other change.

**The security property that makes this safe:** `AgentTaskInput.SystemPromptOverride` is honoured
ONLY on a preview run, and the check lives in the workflow rather than at submission alone. A
prompt is the agent's governing instruction; letting a caller replace it on a committing run would
be an ungoverned way to change what a deployed agent does, bypassing the version history and
promotion ladder that exist to make such a change visible.

The ranking is deliberately not "cheapest wins": governance accuracy first, then pass rate, then
cost as a tiebreak. A prompt that is faster and cheaper but refuses less reliably is a worse
prompt, and shipping it on cost alone is the mistake the comparison exists to prevent. Throughout,
"governance was not measured" is reported as exactly that, never as 0% or 100%.

Migration `00142`. 16 package tests, 18 handler tests, 5 workflow tests, 16 CLI tests, 15 Python
and 13 TypeScript.

### Part A: conversational topic rails ✅ BUILT

`internal/topicrail` decides whether a turn is in scope. A `guard` node whose ref carries the
`rail:` prefix evaluates a named rail; without the prefix it runs the policy check exactly as
before, so existing graphs are untouched.

Detection is tiered: embedding similarity by default (reusing the same embedder the semantic cache
and memory already use), with an optional LLM self-check that runs ONLY inside the ambiguity band.
Imposing a model call on every turn to resolve the small fraction that are genuinely uncertain
would pay the maximum price for the minimum benefit.

- **A rail that cannot be evaluated FAILS CLOSED.** It has not approved anything, and treating an
  outage as approval is the failure the node exists to prevent. Tested, including the case where a
  `blocked` branch exists: that branch handles a refusal, not an outage, and routing an evaluation
  failure down it would let a broken rail look like a working one.
- **Promotion is gated on a measured accuracy score.** Never-benchmarked is refused DISTINCTLY
  from benchmarked-and-poor, because one is a missing measurement and the other a failed one, and
  telling someone their rail "scored too low" when it was never scored sends them after the wrong
  problem. An operator may override with a stated reason, which is logged: a hard block with no
  escape hatch gets worked around by editing the database, which is worse.
- **Editing a rail clears its score**, because the score measured a different rail.
- **Every firing carries its evidence** (matched topic, similarity, which tier resolved it) and is
  sealed with a recomputable hash. Allowed firings are recorded too: a rail that never fires and a
  rail that is never reached look identical if only blocks are stored.

Migration `00143`. 29 package tests, 9 workflow tests, 19 CLI tests, 17 TypeScript and 16 Python.

### A real bug this work exposed in the graph executor

`agentgraph.Graph.Next` returned the unconditional edge ALONGSIDE the matching conditional one,
and the walk takes the first successor. A guard's `when: "blocked"` branch was therefore
**unreachable in any graph that also had a default edge**, which is nearly all of them: the
refusal branch an author wrote would be silently skipped and the run would carry on down the happy
path.

This was in the graph executor built earlier in this plan, and one of its own tests passed over it
because both candidate branches happened to be LLM nodes returning the same mock. Fixed so a
matching conditional edge wins, with the unconditional edges as the fallthrough. Both SDK `next()`
mirrors were updated to match, since a kernel/SDK divergence here is exactly what the parity
manifest test exists to catch, and three test expectations that encoded the old behaviour were
corrected.

### Also fixed

- **A genuinely flaky test**, `trust.TestDelegation_InvalidSignatureRejected`. It "tampered" a
  token by appending a fixed `"AA"`, which is a no-op whenever the signature already ended in
  `"AA"`. Flipping the last character instead is also unreliable, because base64url's final
  character can carry padding bits that decode to the same signature. It now tampers the first
  character of the signature segment, which always contributes significant bits. 10/10 green.
- **CRLF damage from my own earlier patch scripts.** Python's `write_text` translates newlines on
  Windows, so 15 files had been rewritten to CRLF against an LF repo, including
  `internal/domain/deployment.go`. I had previously reported that file as "pre-existing CRLF"; it
  was mine. The four files `gofmt -l` still reports (`agentschedule/*`, `domain/api_key_test.go`)
  are genuine pre-existing alignment drift in files this work did not touch.

### Deliberately not built

The **frontend surfaces** for all three parts: the query builder, the variant comparison view, the
`TopicRailBuilder` and the firings review queue. The plan describes each, and each is real work
against the locked design system rather than a wiring exercise. The APIs, SDKs and CLI groups are
complete without them.

**Gates:** `go build ./...`, `go vet`, `gofmt` clean on everything touched; full backend suite
green; full CLI suite green; `mypy --strict` clean with 457 Python tests; `tsc --noEmit` clean with
357 TypeScript tests.

## Voice s2s tool use, and the ontology reaching the SDKs (2026-09-09)

Three items, from a review of what a native-SDK developer can actually build. Two are implemented
here; the third is a plan amendment because the work itself is weeks.

### Speech-to-speech was unreachable, and would have been tool-less if reached

Two separate defects, found in that order.

**The session advertised no tools.** `beginRealtime` opened every s2s session with
`voice.RealtimeSessionOpts{}`: no instructions, no tools. The bridge could always ROUTE a tool call
through the Supervisor, and `SessionConfig` would serialise a tool list if given one, but nothing
ever populated it. The model was therefore never told a tool existed and never asked for one. A
fast-mode call was a pure talker, silently less capable than the same agent on the text pipeline.
`TelephonyDeps.RealtimeTools` was likewise never set in `main.go`, so any tool call that did arrive
was refused fail-closed.

**And FastMode was never set anywhere.** `SessionClaims.FastMode` was declared and read by the
telephony factory, but no code path ever minted a token carrying it. The s2s path was dead in every
deployment: not merely tool-less, unreachable.

Both are fixed, and the fix derives everything from one source. `RealtimeAgentBinding` resolves the
agent's manifest and builds the session from it: instructions from `spec.personality.system_prompt`,
tools from the Supervisor's own definitions for that agent file. That is deliberately the same
derivation the text path uses, because an allowlist built twice is an agent governed differently
depending on which pipeline a call happened to take, and the difference would only surface in
production. A test asserts the two paths advertise the same set.

The trade is now declared where it belongs: `GovernanceSpec.VoiceFastMode`, not among the voice
capabilities. It is not a grant, it is a deliberate exchange of a governance property (mid-utterance
spoken-PII tokenisation) for latency, and it belongs in the section that is read when governance is
reviewed. It rides the SIGNED session token, so a caller cannot opt themselves into the weaker
pipeline by editing what Twilio sends back; a test splices a fast-mode payload onto another token's
signature and asserts it fails verification.

Every failure path resolves to the fully governed pipeline: an unreadable manifest, an absent
lookup, a binding error. Being wrong towards "off" costs latency; being wrong towards "on" silently
drops a governance property on a live call.

A refusal inside a call returns as TEXT rather than an error, because the model is mid-conversation
with a person on the line and needs something it can say. An error would tear down the bridge and
the call with it. Nothing executed either way.

Exposed to the SDKs as `Governance(voice_fast_mode=True)` / `voice_fast_mode`, so it is declarative,
versioned and promotable like everything else on the manifest. 22 tests.

### The ontology was complete and invisible

`internal/memorydepth` is arguably the most defensible engineering in the platform: a typed,
policy-bearing graph where an entity type carries a sensitivity class, a retention limit and a set
of allowed purposes, so a `PII.email` fact inherits erasure and retention automatically and a
purpose-violating extraction is refused at ingest. Paired with a bitemporal graph (migration
`00120`) it answers "what did the agent believe when it decided?", which is what makes a compliance
replay honest rather than reconstructed.

None of it was reachable from either SDK. The memory surface was inject / delete / search /
store_turn / retrieve_context, and the manifest's `Memory` block was six tuning knobs. A developer
could declare `Governance(purpose=...)`, the enforcement half of purpose-binding, and had no way to
see the graph that purpose governs.

`client.memory_depth` / `client.memoryDepth` now covers the whole surface the backend already
serves: facts, the bitemporal graph with `as_of`, self-editing blocks, document and structured
ingest, selective forgetting, GDPR erasure and reflection.

Two distinctions the types make explicit, because collapsing either would be actively misleading:

- **Valid time versus transaction time.** `valid_from`/`valid_to` say when a fact was true in the
  world; `ingested_at`/`invalid_at` say when the agent believed it. Keeping them apart is what lets
  a replay distinguish "this became false" from "we found out it was never true".
- **Forgetting versus erasure.** Forgetting is memory hygiene by age or confidence. Erasure is a
  legal obligation about one subject. Separate methods, separate endpoints, and erasure refuses a
  missing subject before the call rather than reporting success over nothing.

An ingest that refuses extractions reports a `refused` count rather than raising: a refusal is the
ontology working, and throwing would make correct governance look like a failure.

One naming correction landed with it: the knowledge-graph edge type is `KnowledgeEdge`, not
`GraphEdge`, which already means a control-flow edge in `graph.ts`. Two very different things, and
the TypeScript compiler caught the collision. 35 tests across the two SDKs.

### Computer use: plan amended rather than implemented

§18 added to `docs/Actrone_Governed_Computer_Use_Plan.md`. The plan's architecture is sound and
unchanged; the amendment records what a code-versus-plan pass found.

The finding worth acting on first is not in the plan's scope at all: **the four existing `browser_*`
capability tiers grant actions the runtime cannot perform**. `browserpool` implements `navigate` and
nothing else, so an agent granted `browser_form_submit` has no form-submit action to perform. The
plan correctly leaves `browser_*` untouched so no existing agent silently gains the ability to
click, but that leaves three capabilities advertising behaviour that does not exist, in a codebase
whose entire argument is that a declared grant is enforced. §18.1 proposes collapsing the ladder and
rejecting the deprecated names at parse time.

Also recorded: no SDK method exists for the browser tool that already ships (§18.2); Phase 0's "only
code" is a MediaGuard scan endpoint that does not exist yet, though `authorize_tool()` genuinely does
in both SDKs as the plan claims (§18.3); and what remains open after Phase 1, including that desktop
is not committed and "computer use" reads to buyers as "a computer" rather than "a browser viewport"
(§18.4).

### Also fixed

A second flaky test, `distillflow.TestWorkflow_SubmitPollEvaluate`. It tripped Temporal's deadlock
detector under a loaded machine: the detector measures REAL wall-clock time, so a workflow driven
by instantly-returning mocks can exceed the one-second default without anything blocking. Same
cause and same fix as the graph-executor tests, now behind a shared `newDistillEnv()` helper. A
genuine deadlock still fails, at a minute instead of a second. 5/5 green in isolation before the
fix, so it only ever failed when something else was running, which is exactly the shape of flake
that erodes trust in a suite.

**Gates:** `go build ./...`, `go vet`, `gofmt` clean on everything touched; full backend suite green;
`mypy --strict` clean with 474 Python tests; `tsc --noEmit` clean with 375 TypeScript tests.

---

## BYOF reaches the governed knowledge graph, and a correction (2026-09-09)

### The blank-template gap did not exist

I reported that the Studio wizard had no "start blank" option and that anyone building an agent
unlike the templates had to pick the nearest one and undo it. That was wrong. `VERTICAL_TEMPLATES`
already contains a **"Blank agent"** entry (`id: 'blank'`, empty capabilities, empty connectors,
policy note "No defaults applied"), and `TemplateSelector.choose` already clears everything a
previously-picked template pre-filled, because it applies the blank template's empty defaults
through the same code path.

I built a redundant second blank option before catching it, and reverted all of it: the sentinel
constant, the hand-rolled card, and the wizard's deep-link branch. The component is byte-identical
to what it was.

Two counting errors led there, both from reading a truncated slice of the same file and not
checking the rest: I said five templates, then fourteen; there are **seventeen**. I also said the
wizard has ten steps; `STUDIO_STEPS` has **four** (Template, Persona, Capabilities, Actions &
Connections), and the other components are nested inside them.

What survives is a test file, `template-selection.test.ts`, covering behaviour that genuinely had
no coverage: that the blank starting point exists, that switching to it clears a previous
template's capabilities and connectors, that it does NOT discard a name or budget the author
already typed, and that every non-blank template pre-fills at least one capability. The reset was
the thing worth pinning, since a blank agent silently carrying HR's capabilities would be granted
things its author never chose.

### The knowledge graph is now reachable from inside a BYOF run

This is the real gap from the same review. BYOF-hosted agents already got episodic memory: the
harness declares a memory boundary and the workflow maps `retrieve`/`write` onto the same
`RetrieveContext`/`WriteBack` activities the native loop uses. But the boundary offered only those
two ops, so a framework wanting a fact from the typed graph had to make an out-of-band SDK call
that sat outside the durable step and outside the run's audit.

`MemoryOp` now also carries `facts`, `neighbors` and `write_fact`, routed through a new
`MemoryGraph` activity onto `memorydepth`. The op set is deliberately narrow:

- **Reads and a single fact write only.** These are the graph equivalents of "look something up"
  and "note what I learned", which is what a loop legitimately does.
- **Erase and bulk document ingest are absent, and a test asserts they stay absent.** Both carry
  tenant-wide consequences; reaching them mid-loop would make a model's reasoning the trigger for
  an irreversible data operation. They remain explicit, human-initiated client calls.

Three properties the tests pin:

- **`as_of` is forwarded.** Dropping it would answer with today's graph, which looks correct and
  describes a different world. A malformed value is refused rather than ignored, for the same
  reason.
- **A failed graph op degrades rather than kills the run.** Memory is best-effort in the native
  pipeline too; a framework should continue with less context rather than lose the task.
- **An ontology refusal is distinguishable from an outage.** A refusal comes back as
  `{refused: true, reason: ...}`, not an error. "You were not allowed to remember that" will never
  succeed on retry; "the graph is down" might. Collapsing them would invite a retry loop against a
  governance decision.

Org-shared facts are included in a graph read, so a BYOF agent recalls institutional knowledge the
same way a native one does. Withholding them would make the same agent know less under BYOF for no
governance reason.

On the Python side, `actrone.harness.memory_ops` gives typed builders for all five ops. The wire
already accepted a dict, so a driver could technically have sent one, but only by guessing the
field names; the builders put the shape in one place and refuse a neighbours read with no node or
a fact with no predicate before the round trip.

**Gates:** `go build ./...`, `go vet`, `gofmt` clean; full backend suite green; `mypy --strict`
clean with 488 Python tests; `tsc --noEmit` clean on Control Tower with the agent-studio suite
green.

---

## Final compliance sweep (2026-09-10)

A structural pass over everything built for the Native SDK / A2A compliance plan and the parity
work that followed, checking three separate questions: is it built, is it wired, does it meet
CLAUDE.md.

### Wiring, verified mechanically rather than asserted

- **Activities.** All 19 declared on the `ActivityDependencies` interface are registered with the
  Temporal worker, including the two added late (`EvaluateTopicRail`, `MemoryGraph`). An activity
  declared but unregistered fails at runtime with "unknown activity type", never at build, so this
  was compared as two sorted sets rather than by eye.
- **Routes.** Every exported handler method on the five new handlers resolves to a route in
  `main.go`. The only non-route matches were builder methods (`WithNotifier`), and each of those is
  called during construction.
- **Migrations.** `00139` through `00143` present, no gaps anywhere in the 143-file chain, embedded
  by glob so a new file needs no registration.
- **Reachability.** Every exported symbol in the new packages has a production caller. Eight looked
  orphaned on a naive cross-package grep and seven turned out to be intra-package interface calls
  (`PromotedRules`, `Increment`, `Classify` and so on).
- **The four items the plan recorded as unbuilt** (`agent.test()`, guard nodes, mid-run resume,
  org-wide shadow scan) are each backend-present, routed, and in both SDKs.
- **A2A.** Version negotiation reaches the live handler at `a2a.go:75`, dual card signing is wired,
  the interop gate is build-tagged, 100 package tests pass.

### Two real defects found

**A false claim in a doc comment.** `shadowaudit.Recorder.Buffered()` said it was "exposed for
tests and for an operator checking whether the ceiling is being hit". No operator can reach it:
nothing serves that count over HTTP or metrics, and its only callers are tests. The operator signal
is the `shadowaudit.buffer_full` warning the recorder already emits. The comment now says what is
true.

**258 em dashes across 61 files.** CLAUDE.md bans the em dash absolutely, code comments explicitly
included, and this session's work used them throughout. Replaced by pattern rather than blindly:
a trailing dash that wraps to the next line became a comma, a definition-list entry became a colon
attached to its term with the column alignment preserved, and everything else became a comma. No
en dash was introduced, since that is banned as sentence punctuation too.

The rewrite left exactly one artifact, caught by `gofmt` and a follow-up scan: a continuation line
that began with the dash matched the definition-list pattern with an empty term and became `//:`.
Fixed by moving the colon onto the previous line where the sentence actually breaks. A scan for the
same shape across every rewritten Go and TypeScript file found no others. The 21 Python `#:` lines
a naive version of that scan flagged are Sphinx attribute docs, not damage.

### Checked and found already correct

- **Input bounds (§5.1).** Every request decoder in the new handlers is wrapped in a
  `MaxBytesReader`; no unbounded `json.NewDecoder(r.Body)` survives. Every new package declares
  explicit `Max*` ceilings on the strings and collections it accepts.
- **No emoji, no untracked TODOs** anywhere in the new code.
- **Structured logging.** The new packages carry an explicit dotted `event` field on every log
  line. One handler helper passes the dotted name as the log message instead, which matches the
  dominant convention in that layer (228 sites do it that way against 26 with an explicit field),
  so it was left aligned with its surroundings rather than churned.

### Gates

Backend `go build`, `go vet`, `gofmt` clean and the full suite green. CLI the same. `mypy --strict`
clean with 488 Python tests. `tsc --noEmit` clean with 375 TypeScript tests. Control Tower
typecheck, lint and the agent-studio suite green. Everything uncommitted.
